According to different estimates, nearly 20,000 children live in Estonia, whose parents have difficulty providing daily food. Therefore, free school food is important for many students, as it can be their only warm meal once a day. Many parents are unable to give allowance to their children to buy school lunch. Many children have parents working abroad and may not cook themselves warm meals every day. Thus, the student's success may be reduced because an empty stomach paralyzes thinking and inhibits learning. However, all children are equal and have the right to equal treatment. An empty stomach must not become an obstacle to gaining knowledge and thereby securing a successful future. Warm school food is also necessary to ensure that young people are in good health. Man's eating habits begin in childhood. Many diseases in adulthood begin with unbalanced and irregular diets and the resulting childhood prevalence. The warm school lunch from the first to the 12th grade reduces the body's fatigue, thus helping schoolchildren to avoid fatigue and stress. School food contributes to the development of the right eating habits and is a good way to protect children from junk food. During the government of the Central Party, the state began to financially cover the lunches of pupils in comprehensive school and vocational school. In 2002, the Centre Party established a free school lunch for primary school children under the leadership of the then Minister of Education, Mailis Reps, and in 2006 everyone started receiving free lunch until the ninth grade and school-based vocational students. In 2006, the allowance for school lunch was 64 cents, i.e. 10 kroons in the kroon money. Today it has increased to 78 cents, or about 22%. However, according to Statistics Estonia, food and non-alcoholic beverages prices have increased by more than 30% during the same period. Continuing price increases have put school caterers in a very difficult situation where they have to offer a healthy hot lunch within a very small budget. As the school caterer spends less on one portion this year than in 2006, the portions have become smaller. This is confirmed by the children themselves. In the responses to the survey organised by the Aino Järvesoo Foundation, students said that the school lunches were small and too few vegetables were offered. However, the EUR 78-cent public support alone cannot cover the cost of free school lunches, which means that the municipality and the city budget or the parents will have to pay more. Therefore, support for school lunch should be increased to at least EUR 1 in order to provide balanced school meals. The Centre Party Group has proposed several times in this Chamber to increase the support for school food and to extend it to the gymnasts. There was also a bill that we wanted to extend this support to kindergarten children. However, these proposals have not been supported by coalition partners. Although, in essence, no party opposed the proposals, they have not found support. The pretext for not supporting was the lack of sufficient financial resources. However, today we heard a different rhetoric from the Chair of the Committee on Monetary Affairs; he said that perhaps the school lunch support system should be made needs-based instead.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-14_U671	So next time we come up with this proposal, we will be told that the system is wrong and that it needs to be made needs-based. We will soon find out that we have pensions on a need-based basis. The question here is the priorities of a sitting government. After all, healthy children are the basis for the Estonian state's survival. Nor should the financing of school food be viewed as a strict budgetary cost. Proper nutrition as a child ensures healthier working people, and in this case public spending on the payment of incapacity for work and other benefits will be reduced in the future. Active workers, however, ensure higher taxes on labour, which, in turn, helps to rebalance the state budget. Thus, the provision of free school meals from primary school to upper secondary school is indispensable for both short-term and long-term goals. One of the objectives of the social policy of the Republic of Estonia is to reduce poverty in families with children. By offering hot food at least once a day, we ensure the healthy development of children and thus the sustainability of the country. ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-14_U674	Ladies and gentlemen, I want to talk about a subject that, either way and quite directly, relates to the state budget and not just to next year's expenditure. We are talking about border agreements between Estonia and Russia. When you analyse the border between Russia and Estonia for a few centuries, you wonder how much it has changed in time. It has been six and a half years since the last attempt to establish a contractual limit between the two countries. I was then chairman of the Foreign Affairs Committee of the Riigikogu and I remember well what was thought of in the political groups at the time and why the Preamble was added to the Border Treaty. In fact, this is far from exceptional. In the United States, it is quite common for the Congress to add explanatory introductions to complement the ratification laws for its people. This is how we acted at the urgent request of the ambassadors of the Fatherland Union, adding a reference to the Tartu Peace Treaty in the introductory text of the Treaty. Should we have stopped referring to the peace of Tartu? Professor Tanel Kerikmäe, a lawyer, distinguishes two aspects here. First of all, at the border, if such an agreement has already taken place before and there are no distinct views. It would be wrong to accuse anyone of selling behind Narva, as the Riigikogu did not contest the outcome of previous negotiations over the new official border, which were included in the infamous Preamble Border Treaty. I quote Mr. Church Mountains. On the other hand, the question raised could mean additional conditions. Kerikmäe continues to take the view that the fact that Tartu's peace is not mentioned in the preamble to the Ratification Act does not in any way undermine the validity of an international agreement that is important to us, but rather alters the scope of its validity by means of the mandate given in the Constitution. The peace of Tartu is not just a border treaty. The Estonian Foreign Minister Urmas Paet signed the border agreement with his Russian colleague Sergei Lavrov, which then had no introductory text, in Moscow in May 2005. The Riigikogu ratified the Estonian-Russian border treaty with the Preamble in June of the same year, even then 4 other members of the Riigikogu opposed the decision. Pretty soon, to our surprise, Moscow was announced that the introduction to the border treaty could be an introduction to Estonia's territorial demands on Russia's territories. Of course, we have always rejected these accusations at all levels. Moreover, despite Moscow's arguments, Estonia has never changed a word in the texts of the border treaties. After all, the Estonian-Russian border agreement signed in 2005 was not ratified by Russia because the Estonian Riigikogu added a preamble to it when it came into force, which stated that the border agreement would partly change the line of the state line set out in the Tartu Peace Treaty of 2 February 1920, but would not affect the rest of the Tartu Peace Treaty or determine the treatment of bilateral issues not covered by the border agreement. A month ago, the Riigikogu Committee on Foreign Affairs proposed that all political groups and non-groups members of the Riigikogu turn to the Estonian Government with a joint letter in order to resume negotiations on the border agreement. The proposal was supported and the government decided to take the matter seriously. The Estonian Minister of Foreign Affairs met with his colleague in Luxembourg on 14 October. Consultations were agreed upon. A meeting between the foreign ministries of two countries took place in Moscow on 31 October. Estonia was represented by the Deputy Chancellor of Legal and Consular Affairs of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs Lauri Bambus and Estonian Ambassador to Moscow Jüri Luik. The representatives of Estonia base their consultations on the Riigikogu's proposal to conclude a border agreement satisfactory for both parties to the government. The consultations do not intend to change the border line contained in the border agreements agreed in 2005. Both parties to the negotiations consider that both Estonia and Russia need a border agreement. Tanel Kirikmäe believes that Estonia should conclude a new border agreement, but certainly not on every condition. The consultations that have begun are a sign of the will between the two countries. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs will certainly try to do its best and will be guided by the principles adopted earlier, such as the recommendation of the former Chancellor of Justice that the new border must be even partially identical to the post-Tartu peace border. This requirement stems from the wording of the Constitutional Regulation. At the end of the consultations, the Riigikogu, we, has the opportunity to assess the suitability of the border agreement. The question of the Preamble is obviously not addressed at least in the legal key for consultations, because it is not the competence of our negotiators. At the meeting of the Riigikogu Foreign Affairs Committee on Thursday, we analysed the first meeting with Russian representatives in Moscow, together with Deputy Chancellor Lauri Bambus and Foreign Minister Urmas Paet, and discussed border agreements in a wider context. Consultations, which are likely to continue in Tallinn in December, will seek to reach a common position on the wording by which, while working further, we can reach the enforcement and ratification of border agreements in the parliament of two countries. At our level, we will continue to work here in this building, in the Riigikogu. Last week Mikhail Margelov, Chairman of the Foreign Affairs Committee of the Russian Federation Council, visited Tallinn, who has been a known colleague for years. In addition to the issue of border agreements, we addressed the strengthening of cross-border cooperation. To this end, a number of infrastructure objects, such as Narva's new bridge, should be built. This would greatly contribute to the acceleration of border crossings and increase opportunities for tourism and trade. Mikhail Margelov was of the same opinion and proposed to continue the contacts of MEPs in Moscow next spring. Of course, we also want closer working contacts with the Foreign Affairs Committee of the State Duma and its Chairman, Aleksei Pushkov. Border agreements do not need to rush, but their existence would provide a number of opportunities to increase the number of contacts not too close between the two neighbouring countries. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-14_U686	Honourable Chairman of the Riigikogu! Good tradesmen, guests and internet monitors! In my speech, I will focus mainly on our amendment, which bears number 78 and talks about the price of the room and the livelihood of the people. The country has many important functions today, the most important of which is to ensure decent living conditions for all its inhabitants. Estonia belongs to the European Union and the euro area, but our people's standard of living is clearly lower than that of many Western Europeans. Unlike wealthier nations and countries, rising food prices and rising housing costs continue to be a problem in Estonia. In recent years, the situation has deteriorated even further, as the inevitable expenditure on food and housing has clearly increased faster than people's incomes. At this point, the state has a moral obligation to intervene and help its people. Housing costs are, as we know, the greatest in the winter that is about to come. We have to pay rising heating costs. Thus, reducing the cost of indoor heating is the best way to keep housing costs under control. Unfortunately, today's government coalition has so far acted in the opposite direction and adopted a number of draft laws that have actually increased people's heating costs. You will probably remember that heat was subject to a VAT rate of 5% until 1 July 2007. The VAT Act, which introduced a VAT rate on heat energy of 18%, then entered into force. Shortly after the economic crisis in 2009, the government coalition decided to increase the VAT rate to 20% at very short notice and also to increase the rate of excise duty on natural gas to 367 kroons per 1000 cubic metres. As a result of these decisions, the monthly heating costs of all people living in central heating homes and gas-fired apartments or private homes increased. Even worse timing is quite difficult to imagine, but during the economic crisis, the only goal was to achieve a balance in the state budget. People's budgets were private and clearly secondary. The most difficult effect is, of course, the high room temperature price for people with lower than average incomes, whose monthly budget makes up a much larger share of heating costs than for wealthier people. In this respect, despite growth and the alleged increase in well-being, heating costs have also increased more quickly in recent years than people's wages or pensions. According to Statistics Estonia, thermal energy has increased by as much as 12.1% compared to October last year. I wonder how this number goes with an election promise to keep home expenses under control. In the budget of the average Estonian person, housing costs now account for one major share, reaching up to 20%. A person earning a minimum wage of EUR 290, who, for example, lives in a two-room panelhouse apartment, will spend half of his income on utilities during the heating period. This example is given in the Mustamäe district. During winter, unavoidable heating costs mostly account for the bulk of the apartment's utility costs. High heating costs are not a minor problem that solves itself. Ask a member of the management board of a building management company how the winter heating period affects the payment of people's utilities. In households with lower incomes, it is already difficult to find money to buy food for life alongside a mortgage or rent payments taken to buy a home. Winter heating costs are often the last blow that leads to a final imbalance in the family budget, a deficit. Due to the debts of utility bills, a large number of people have had to give up their homes, which is a serious disgrace to one of them, and at least to a successful country in words. We must do everything we can to value and support every person in Estonia and avoid situations like this with a tragic end. According to the vision of the Estonian Centre Party, the state must not sit back, but must find ways to alleviate the situation of homeowners. That is why we have repeatedly proposed lowering room-warm VAT to 9%. Unfortunately, this draft has not been supported and has been consistently rejected. Unfortunately, the Government of the Republic has not put forward any substantive arguments against the draft legislation on VAT in our room. The fear that people who do not really need it will also benefit from a reduced rate recalls the rhetoric of the government coalition in changing the child benefit system. The result of the last much-accepted reform is that child support will remain at the absurdly low level of the majority of families next year, which, in the light of the continuing price increase, will essentially lead to a reduction. Just as child support must not be poverty support, the need-based slogan should not be used to combat the lowering of people's heating bills, as the cost of housing for most Estonian residents is a considerable departure and their growth is a very serious problem. The second argument that has been put forward for consumers against a proposal to lower the price of heat energy is, in my opinion, even less critical. In its letter to the Committee on Monetary Affairs of August 2011, the Government found that the general support for heat through the tax system favours over-consumption and does not encourage saving and saving the environment. Finally, I call on the government departments to be more guided by people's well-being in their activities and at least also to discuss the proposals of opposition parties, which today, by the way, are supported by the majority of the Estonian people. Thank you for your attention.

Night sittings are a common method in most Western parliaments against the overwhelming voting machine of the government alliances. The first reason for the night hearing is attitude. If the debate is not recommended and if no proposal is heard, then the logical conclusion is the night hearing. Night sessions were born, by the way, in the Roman Senate, where they became a Democratic weapon against dictators. Senator Cato the Younger tried to prevent Caesar's journey to power and the Republic from becoming a personal cult empire with lingering speeches. Although Caesar was still in power, night sittings were considered such an effective means that they were still part of Parliament's work. Night sessions are an effective weapon against those who think they have a monopoly on the truth. This is the only working weapon in a situation where the majority force does not want to hold a normal political debate. The proposal that we are trying to make visible and audible at night sessions is a reduction of electricity excise duty four and a half times, i.e. to the minimum permitted by European Union rules. On TV, the choice of a better and cheaper electricity package may be advertised, but the upcoming price increase will also have a rather difficult effect on the economy, as confirmed by Eesti Pank, giving a significant share of the forecast increase in the total cost of living for the following year. The task of the Centre Party is to remind the government that, for example, those who have electric heating at home will find it very difficult to have a new year without a fall in excise duty. Why isn't the government defending these people's interests? It should be taken into account that rising electricity prices also affect the competitiveness of the economy. Production and servicing at an acceptable price is becoming increasingly difficult. Estonia's business would benefit from the reduction of electricity excise duty. If nothing is done about the increase in electricity prices, the business environment in Estonia will deteriorate, the chances of creating jobs will be reduced. Three more minutes, please.
